The following sections list the components of a Basic program, along with some examples and descriptions on how they operate.

In this section the various components are listed alphabetically. The explanation of each term consists of 3 parts:

1. Purpose . . . . . Explains when the function is used.

2. Format . . . . . Shows how the function is used.

3. Example . . . . Gives a practical application.


---

---
title: "9 Programming a controller"
order: 590
---

This section explains the programming steps to setup a panel for a controls application. The steps are presented here in a somewhat logical order but the system is flexible, you can complete the sections you have information on as the data comes in and finish off the other parts later. It is assumed that at this point, the T3000 has been setup as per instructions in Chapters 3 and 8.

1. The Input and Output List

List all the inputs and outputs for the system to be controlled. A spreadsheet is generally available from the original project description. If you are starting from scratch, list the equipment and for each of these work out the list of inputs and outputs. Be sure to check the sequence of operations for the system, there's often wording in the text which doesn't match up well with the drawings, those few words can be expensive words which require extra sensors and i/o for your project.

Assuming you are doing your own list, its OK to proceed as information comes in, you can always revise the list as the project proceeds. The points don't have to be in any particular sequential order but its definitely best to keep related i/o near others on the same equipment. And certainly its best to keep i/o from one air handler for example, on one controller so that if the network goes down the equipment can still operate in stand alone mode. Software can connect any i/o to other i/o in the system but its best to keep the list in logical order.

-Once the list is together its time to assign some names to the i/o for entering into the system. In the T3000 Building Automation System we can we can assign each input or output with two names, a long one which is 20 characters for showing in reports and user screens, and a shorter one which is 8 characters to be used in the programming.

- Each input and output needs to be assigned to a physical input or output point on the controller. For inputs this is easy, any input can be jumper configured to match up with most any sensor, switch, transducer or meter. For the outputs, you need to assign the analog items to one of the analog outputs. On the binary outputs to enable fans and pumps the job is a little easier, assign each of these to an output relay on the controller.

TIP: If you need more binary outputs, a spare analog output can be converted to a binary output with an external 12VC coil relay, the analog output will drive the relay to gain one extra really from an analog output. The reverse is not true though, relays are fixed as binary outputs.

- The input list should show the sensor or transducer type and jumper settings.

- For valves and dampers especially, there are many arrangements for the i/o depending on the equipment and project specs so its best to have a data sheet for the exact item. The valves will be specified as normally open or closed, gather that information from the mechanical drawings and sequence description. The output signal can be floating control, modulating or on/off, some have feedback signal as well. All this info is in the data sheets for the actuator and is important to have on hand when laying out the system.

2. Configure “System” Components

Run the T3000 software and Sign-On with your Name and Password. Setup the T3000 as per instructions in Chapters 3 and 8.

3. Program Inputs

“Inputs” are the sensors and devices which are connected into the T3000 panel so that the T3000 can be programmed to act upon. For more information on In puts see section 6.3.

Select “Inputs” from the CONTROL menu, press ALT-I, or type INS from the command line.

Enter in a description that will identify each point. Each point must be unique or the system will not accept the description. When entering the 20 character “Description” as it is known, try to be as descriptive as possible. You should also develop a consistent nomenclature for the various types of control points such as “RTS” in front of all room temperature sensors. Using this nomenclature, the room sensor in office 102 would be named “RTS OFFICE102".

Also enter the “Label” which is the eight character short hand name for the input point which will be used in the Control Basic programs. Again, each LABEL must be unique or the system will not accept the name. By using the above nomenclature, the label for the same office temperature sensor would be “RTS102 ”.

<table>
  <tr><td>Other Examples:</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Input Point</td><td></td><td>Description</td><td>Label</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Air Handling Unit 1</td><td>Supply Air Temperature</td><td>AHU1 SUPPLY AIR TEMP</td><td>AHU1.SAT</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Air Handling Unit 1</td><td>Supply Fan Status</td><td>AHU1 SUPPLY FAN STAT</td><td>AHU1.S</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Pump # 1 Radiation Loop Start/Stop</td><td>P1 RAD LOOP S/S</td><td>P1.S/S</td></tr>
</table>

Select the range:

To set the range high light the field, press ENTER, and enter the desired range number. If no standard range will work with the sensor, see the section of this manual describing the construction of a “TABLE”.

The “D” field is a simple check mark field to verify the correct operation of each sensor. Toggle the field back to normal, check for correct readings, and enter calibration values if required.

Your changes are sent to the panel automatically if you leave the current screen, but to see the effect of current changes, you must Send and then Load the Input changes.

![](media/images-drex_module_11_custom.png)

4. Program Outputs

“Outputs” are the output signals from the T3000 sent to the fans, pumps, boilers, etc. which are connected to the T3000. Outputs can be either analog or digital. The software setting determines which mode the output will work with. The engineering units for each output can be configured to match the actual units normally associated with the device being controlled ( i.e. Stop/Start , Close/Open, or % Open, % Closed, CFM). For more information on “Out-puts” see section 5.2.

To get to the outputs screen, type ALT-O or Select “Outputs” from the CONTROL menu. Alternatively, you can type OUTS from the command line.

Just as was described for the inputs, each OUT PUT re quires a 20 character description and an 8 character label. The names should follow a naming nomenclature similar to that used for the inputs, and each name must be unique.

To set the range highlight the field, press ENTER, and enter the desired range.

Enter in the Low and High volt ages for any analog devices such as valves or dampers which operate over 2-10 VDC range rather than the normal 0-10V range.

Enter starts delays as required. This is used for digital out puts only and de lays the start of a device in minutes: seconds up to a maximum delay of 99 minutes 50 seconds. A value entered in this field superimposes a lag on all other control actions such as weekly routines or basic programs which govern this point.

If you want to increase the security level on the output, enter that level in the “S” field.

![](media/images-drex_module_11_custom_2.png)

Toggle all outputs to be decommissioned. Later, as you verify their correct operation toggle the field back to normal.

When satisfied with their operation, Send and then Load the Outputs.

5. Define programming jobs based on out puts that you have to control

For example, a panel may have the following control jobs.

- Boiler Control

- Chiller Control

- Fan System 1 Control

Select “Programs” from the Control menu, or by typing PRGS from “Direct Access”. Enter in the program description and label. Each point must be unique or the system will not accept the description. When entering the description try to be as descriptive as possible.

For example: Air Handling Unit 1 Control = AHU1 PROGRAM

![](media/images-drex_module_11_custom_3.png)

6. Write Individual Programs

Press the “Ins” key while highlighting the line of the de sired program, or using “Direct Access” type the program's label. As you write a program you will want to make use of 4 other point types:

- Variables - VARS

- Controllers - CONS

- Weekly Routines - WRS

- Annual Routines - ARS

Set up these points as the need for them arises. When finished writing your program, send the program by pressing “F2". This will first check the program for errors. If correct, it will send the program to the panel. If not, it will supply an error message complete with line numbers. For more information on Control Basic Programming see chapter 10.

7. Define Graphic Screens:

Graphic Screens are used to group points of all types into one logical unit for displaying on the screen. The elements of a group can be displayed over a graphic picture to augment the display of information.

Select “Graphic” from the Control menu. Enter in the name and label. Each label must be unique or the system will not accept the description. When entering the label try to be as descriptive as possible.

![](media/images-drex_module_11_custom_4.png)

8. Design screens:

Press the “Ins” key while highlighting the line of the desired screen.

To display a point on the screen, move the cursor to the desired location and press the “Ins” key. A pop- up box will appear on the screen to let you specify the element to be added to the display. A second pop- up menu will appear which controls the colour and size of the element text, and several other functions associated with this element. Press the “Esc” key to exit pop- up menu. Continue to add points to display until completion. When satisfied with the display go to File and Save.

For a more detailed description on building Screens see section 6.1.

9. Define Monitors

There are two types of monitors: Ana log which trends analog points, and Digital which logs start and stop times of digital points. Both are displayed together on a single monitor from the Data pull-down menu.

Set up monitors by entering the monitor name and entering the points to be monitored. Enter the interval time between samples, and then Send. Sampling of these points will now take place. If for some reason you decide to change some aspect of a monitor, it will erase all previous data and start a new one once you send the changes to the panel. For more information on Monitors see section 7.1.

10. Save the Program

Using the “File” selection on the Program menu, “Save” the panel program. Steps 1 through 9 are sent to the panel each time that you do a “Send”. By “Saving” the program, the program is saved in your PC and is available to be down loaded back into the panel if the panel crashes or loses its program.


---

---
title: "Control Basic Programming"
order: 600
---




---

---
title: "Numbers, Time and Date Handling"
order: 610
---

The following sections list the control basic programming language, it is suited for real time control applications with many advanced features built into the language directly which allows the programmer to create powerful programs with a small number of statements. Along with the description of the statements are some short examples for each.

The list of program statements and commands is listed alphabetically. Each item consists of 3 parts:

1. Purpose . . . . . Explains when the function is used.

2. Format . . . . . Shows how the function is used.

3. Example . . . . Gives a short example program

## 10.4.1 REAL NUMBERS

Real numbers between +/- 3.4*10^38 are recognized. The number can be entered in a number of different formats.

Examples: 0.00000123 , 7.879E-12 , 100

## 10.4.2 DAYS

Each of the 7 weekdays evaluates to a real number as shown below:

SUN = 0 MON = 1 TUE = 2 WED = 3 THU = 4 FRI = 5 SAT = 6

Example:10 IF DOW = 1 THEN OPEN SPRINKLER1

20 IF TIME-ON (SPRINKLER1 ) > 0:05:00 THEN CLOSE SPRINKLER1

This example will open a sprinkler valve on Monday morning at 12:00:00 and turn it off at 12:05:00

## 10.4.3 DATES ( JAN 1 to DEC 31 )

[TBD] Dates can be referred to in programming statements like a real number which allow logic operations on calendar events for certain days of the year, AUG12 and NOV10 can be used in the program statements.

The month abbreviations are:

JAN, FEB, MAR, APR, MAY, JUN, JUL, AUG, SEP, OCT, NOV, DEC.

Note that February is allowed to have 29 days as happens during a leap year rather than the usual 28 days.

Example:10 IF DOY = AUG12 THEN STOP FAN

## 10.4.4 TIMES ( 0:0:0 to 23:59:59 )

Times are evaluated to real numbers using the following formula:

time = hour*100 + minutes + seconds / 100

The time of 14:30:30 is returned as a real number of value = 1430.30

If the seconds are not specified, the time is interpreted as hours and minutes only, ie 12:30 returns a value of 1230

EXAMPLE: IF TIME = 13:00:05 THEN START PUMP1

See the
TIME
command for more examples of math using time

## 10.4.7 COMMANDS AND FUNCTIONS

As sown in the previous pages, a Basic program is comprised of Basic commands and functions. The commands and functions operate in conjunction with a mixture of mathematical operators, variables, days, dates, times and real numbers to accomplish a given control sequence.

COMMANDS

Every control basic line must have at least one command. If more than one command is on one line, they must be separated by semicolons.

FUNCTIONS

functions represent values that are dependent on 1 or more other values or some system constant such as time. They can be used in any statement where it calls for an expression.

In this section the Commands and Functions are listed alphabetically. The explanation of each term consists of 3 parts:

1. Purpose . . . . . Explains when the function is used.

2. Format . . . . . Shows how the function is used.

3. Example . . . . Gives a practical application.


---

---
title: "Logical Operators"
order: 620
---

## 10.4.5 MATHEMATICAL OPERATORS

Mathematical Operators are discussed below in their order of priority. Operators of higher priority are executed before operators of lower priority. Within the same group, operators have the same priority and are evaluated left to right. The order of execution can be modified by the use of parenthesis. The general form for the use of operators is:

expression operator expression

Note that NOT has a unique form as shown in the next section.

NOT

NOT is a unique operator in that it only acts on one value which must be a variable. (All other operators act on 2 expressions). NOT per forms a logical not operation on an expression. If the expression is 0 the result is 1. If the expression is non-zero the result is 0.

Example:
10 IF NOT OUT1 THEN STOP OUT2

20 IF NOT ( OUT1 OR OUT2) THEN START OUT3

“^ ” (T3000 only)

The ^ symbol is used to raise a value to a power. Press shift and 6 on the PC keyboard.

Example:
10 A = IN1 ^ VAR1

* , / , \ , MOD

These operators perform multiplication and division.

* Multiplication.

/ Division.

<table>
  <tr><td>\</td><td>The integer portion of a division</td><td>(ie: 13 \ 5 = 2).</td></tr>
  <tr><td>MOD</td><td>The remainder of a division</td><td>(ie: 13 MOD 5 = 3 ).</td></tr>
</table>

+ , -

These operators perform addition and subtraction.

&lt; , > , &lt;= , >= , &lt;>

These operators per form comparisons. The result is 1 if the comparison is true, otherwise the result is 0.

<table>
  <tr><td>&lt;</td><td>Result is true if left expression is LESS THAN right expression.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>&gt;</td><td>Result is true if left expression is GREATER THAN right expression</td></tr>
  <tr><td>&lt;=</td><td>Result is true if left expression is LESS THAN OR EQUAL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>to right expression.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>&gt;=</td><td>Result is true if left expression is GREATER THAN OR EQUAL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>to right expression.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>=</td><td>Result is true if both expressions are EQUAL.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>&lt;&gt;</td><td>Result is true if expressions are NOT EQUAL.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>10.4.6 <strong>LOGIC</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td><strong>AND</strong></td></tr>
</table>

AND per forms the logical “and” of the two expressions. The result is true (1) if both expressions are non-zero, otherwise the result is false (0).

OR

OR per forms the logical “or” of the two expressions. The result is true (1) if either or both expressions are non-zero, otherwise the result is false (0).

XOR

XOR performs the logical “exclusive or” of the two expressions. The result is true (1) if the two expressions are different, otherwise the result is false (0).


---

---
title: "Expressions"
order: 630
---

## 10.4.9 EXPRESSIONS

Expressions evaluate real number. They can be a combination of the following:

<table>
  <tr><td>Variables</td><td></td><td>Functions</td><td>Operators</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Real Numbers</td><td>Days</td><td>Dates</td><td>Times</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Examples:</td><td>10</td></tr>
  <tr><td>IN3</td></tr>
  <tr><td>10 + IN3</td></tr>
  <tr><td>A + IN3 - SQR( ABS( VAR12 ) ) * 12.3 / TUE</td></tr>
</table>


---

---
title: "Variables"
order: 640
---

VARIABLES

Variables as used in Control Basic consist of conventional variables, and points.

LOCAL VARIABLES

There are 26 local variables available to each program. These variables can assume any floating point value. The current value of a local variable can be determined by using the PRINT command (i.e.: PRINT A). Local variables are unique to each program and cannot be used anywhere else in the system. The symbols used for local variables are the characters of the alphabet (i.e.: A,B,C...Z).

POINTS

The 10 point types are variables because they have a value. This value may be accessed, and some points are set through Control Basic. The table below summarizes the fields of each point that can be read or written to:

<table>
  <tr><td><strong>POINT TYPE</strong></td><td><strong>MNEMONIC</strong></td><td><strong>READ FIELD</strong></td><td><strong>WRITE FIELD</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>Outputs</td><td>OUT1 thru OUT128 Output</td><td>Output</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Inputs</td><td>IN1 thru IN128</td><td>Input</td><td>Input</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Variables</td><td>VAR1 thru VAR128 Value</td><td></td><td>Value</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Controllers</td><td>CON1 thru CON64</td><td>Output</td><td>Setpoint Value</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Weekly Routines</td><td>WR1 thru WR32</td><td>Output</td><td>n/a</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Annual Routines</td><td>AR1 thur AR8</td><td>Output</td><td>n/a</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Programs</td><td>PRG1 thru PRG128</td><td>Status</td><td>Status</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Control Groups</td><td>GRP1 thru GRP32</td><td>n/a</td><td>n/a</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Digital Monitors</td><td>DMON1 thru DMON128</td><td>On Time</td><td>n/a</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Analog Monitors</td><td>AMON1 thru AMON96</td><td>S(Status)</td><td>S</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Array Elements</td><td>AY1[x] thru AY48[x]</td><td>Value</td><td>Value</td></tr>
</table>

All point type variables can be referred to by their label. Some statements and functions would be illogical using certain points. If an illogical operation is attempted, nothing will happen.

Examples:
START WR1 (cannot start Weekly Routines in BASIC)

DMON1 = 23 * A. (DMONS cannot be set by BASIC)

  <tr><td>* / \ MOD</td><td>OPERATOR</td><td>Multiplication, division, integer portion of a division, remained of a division</td></tr>

</table>

<table>
  <tr><td>AND</td><td>LOGIC</td><td>Logical AND, both conditions must be TRUE for the expression to evaluate to TRUE</td></tr>
  <tr><td>OR</td><td>LOGIC</td><td>Logical OR, if either expression is TRUE the result is TRUE</td></tr>
  <tr><td>XOR</td><td>LOGIC</td><td>Logical Exclusive OR, evalutates to TRUE if the two expressions are different</td></tr>

  <tr><td><a href="../../../../../../QN/temcocontrols/temco-platform/control-basic-programming/alarm-alarm-at.md">ALARM-AT</a></td><td>COMMAND</td><td>Selects which panel will annunciate alarms, default is ALL panels</td></tr>
  <tr><td>AVG</td><td>FUNCTION</td><td>Average of all numbers enclosed in the brackets following this command</td></tr>

  <tr><td>CONRATE</td><td>FUNCTION</td><td>Allows a Basic program to up date the derivative term in a particular PID controller</td></tr>
  <tr><td>CONRESET</td><td>FUNCTION</td><td>Allows a Basic program to up date the integral term in a particular PID controller</td></tr>
  <tr><td>DALARM</td><td>COMMAND</td><td>Delayed alarm, allows generation of alarms if a condition is true for a certain time</td></tr>

  <tr><td>GOSUB</td><td>COMMAND</td><td>Branches to sub routine, executes program till next RE TURN statement, and loops back</td></tr>

  <tr><td>START</td><td>COMMAND</td><td>Sets the value of an items to 1, same as OUT1 = 1 or OPEN OUT1</td></tr>

</table>

title: "ABS"

ABS

<table>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>Returns the absolute value of an expression.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>ABS( expression )</td></tr>

</table>

---
title: "Annual routines"
order: 660
---

A weekly schedule repeats every seven days, which covers a shop that opens at nine every morning. It does not cover the two weeks in August when it does not open at all. An annual routine is the second calendar: a list of dates that are the same every year, applied on top of the weekly schedules.

It is also where the year's holidays live, which is why the screen is behind the **Holidays** command in the Control menu (`Alt - H`).

## What a marked day means

A day marked in the annual routine is treated as an exception. On that date the panel follows the holiday behaviour instead of the weekly schedule — the same behaviour the panel would use on a day it was told to stay closed.

Two kinds of date are worth keeping apart when you plan a year:

| Kind | Example | Where it belongs |
|---|---|---|
| The same date every year | 1 January, 25 December | an annual routine |
| A date that moves every year | Easter, a maintenance shutdown | an annual routine, edited when the year changes |
| The same day every week | Sunday | a weekly schedule |

A moving holiday is the case that catches people out: it is an annual routine, but it has to be adjusted each year because the panel stores dates, not rules.

## The screen

The annual routine for a panel is a calendar of the whole year, not a list you type into. A day is marked by clicking it in the twelve-month view; the same click removes the mark.

- **Add/Not Add** — the context menu on a day, which is how a single date is set or cleared without a dialog.
- **Clear All** — clears the panel's calendar in one step, before a year is planned from scratch.
- **Edit Calendar Day** — the same edit, opened from a BACnet device's own screen rather than from T3000's calendar.
- **Add Log** — records the date in the panel's table.
- **Write & Read back** — sends the calendar to the panel and reads it back, which is the way to confirm what the panel actually holds.

## Leap years

The calendar holds 366 days, so 29 February exists whether or not the current year has one. A panel set up in a leap year keeps the date; in a year without 29 February it is simply never reached. Nothing has to be re-entered, and nothing shifts by a day.

## Reading the calendar back

A calendar that was written but never read back is a calendar you are guessing about. **Write & Read back** is the button that turns the screen into evidence: if the marks come back the way they were set, the panel has them; if they do not, the write did not land and the panel will run its schedules as though nothing was marked.

That matters most before a long holiday, which is exactly when nobody is on site to notice.

## Related

- [Schedules](control/schedules.md) — the weekly calendar this one overrides.
- [Holidays](control/holidays.md) — the screens in the control chapter.

A TStat8 has its own annual routine table, set from its tree the same way: right-click the `AR1` log and add the dates.


---

---
title: "ALARM, ALARM-AT"
order: 670
---

ALARM

Purpose: To generate alarms based on user defined conditions and includes automatic hysterisis to reduce multiple triggers from the same event.

Format: ALARM x > y , z , string

ALARM x < y , z , string

Description: x > y and x < y are the alarm conditions

z is the alarm hysterisis, once the alarm is triggered the condition must swing back by this amount before another event is triggered.

string is the alarm message with a length of up to 69 characters.

Example:
10 ALARM SAT > SETPOINT + 5 , 2 , SUPPLY AIR TEMP IS TOO HIGH

In this example, the alarm is triggered when the supply air is 5 degrees above setpoint. Another alarm will not be triggered till the alarm resets itself by swinging thru 3 Degrees again ( 5 - 2 = 3 degrees) over setpoint.

ALARM- AT

<table>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>Selects which panel(s) will receive the alarms.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>ALARM-AT panel1 panel2 ...] or ALARM-AT ALL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Example:</td><td>10 ALARM-AT ALL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>20REM ALARMS WILL GO TO ALL PANELS.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>30ALARM-AT 2 8</td></tr>
  <tr><td>40REM ALARMS WILL GO TO PANELS 2 AND 8 ONLY</td></tr>
</table>


---

title: "AVG"
order: 680
---

AVG

<table>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>Returns the average of a series of numbers</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>AVG( expression , expression , ... )</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td>Note:</td><td>A space is needed before and after each comma.</td></tr>
</table>

Example:

10 AVGTEMP = AVG( RMTEMP1 , RMTEMP2 , RMTEMP3 )

---
title: "CALL"
order: 690
---

[TBD] This statement not implemented yet

CALL

Purpose: To call a program with a list of arguments to be used in that program. Used in conjunction with DECLARE

Format: CALL program = [ arg1 , arg2 ...]

Description: program is the particular program to call, ie PRG1 or PRG2 for example.

= arg1, arg2 are arguments to be passed to the program

Note:
Arguments may be any point, local variable, or expression and the '=' sign is necessary before the list of args.

Up to 40 arguments may be listed.

Programs can be nested up to 10 levels using CALL and DECLARE.

Example:
10 CALL PRG1 = SATZ1 , 21 , SF1 , CON1 , 18.5 , 22.8 , VALVE1

The values of each of the arguments get passed to PRG1. PRG1 uses them and may change the values if the arguments are not constants.

---
title: "DALARM"
order: 720
---

Purpose:
To program delayed alarms, a condition must exist for a certain amount of time before the alarm is generated. A new alarm will not be created till the condition returns to normal and then returns to alarm at which time the delay timer starts again.

Format:
DALARM expression , num , string

Description: expression is the alarm condition

num is the number of seconds to delay before causing an alarm

string is the alarm message with a length of up to 69 characters.

Example:
10 DALARM FAN1 AND NOT FAN1_ST , 60 ,FAN1 NOT COMING ON

20 DALARM NOT FAN1 AND FAN1_ST , 60 ,FAN1 TURNED ON MANUALLY

---
title: "Declare"
order: 730
---

[TBD] This statement not implemented yet

<table>
  <tr><td><strong>DECLARE</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>To receive a list of arguments into a program.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Used in conjunction with CALL.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>DECLARE [ var1 , var2 ... ]</td></tr>
</table>

Description: var1, var2, ... are variables (i.e. A, B, VAR1)

Example:
10 DECLARE SAT SAS FAN CTRL MIN-TEMP MAX-TEMP VALVE 100 END

Notes:

1. DECLARE must be used on the first line in the program. Using DECLARE makes the program a “subroutine” and should only be run from a CALL statement.

2. The variables are set to the value of the corresponding arguments in the CALL statement.

3. Use END statement to end the program.

4. When END statement is executed, the values of the variables are copied back to the original arguments in the CALL statement.

5. [TBD] Disable the “Timer” field of a program using DECLARE so that it will not run unless called by CALL.

![](../media/images-drex_module_12_10_custom.png)

6. CALL and DECLARE are useful for keeping a program modular. The logic only has to be written once and can be used by many systems by calling the routine with different arguments for each system.

title: "ENABLE"
order: 760
---

Purpose:
Sets the value of a variable or other item to 1.

Also can be used to turn a binary variable or output ON

Format:
ENABLE variable

Example:
10 ENABLE FAN101

20 ENABLE A

30 ENABLE HEATMODE


---

---
title: "END"
order: 770
---

Purpose:
Ends execution of a program. Used to control flow of logic through a given program.

Format:
END

Example
:
100 REM CHECK IF THE BOILERS ARE RUNNING

110 IF NOT BOILER-S THEN END

120 REM ONLY EXECUTE THE NEXT LINES IF THE BOILERS ARE RUNNING

130 START PUMP101

Programming tip:

GOTO statements, END statements and similar jumps in the basic programming language can lead to code which is hard to debug.

Logic with a lot of GOTOS and jumps is referred to as spaghetti code, use these statements sparingly.

The above example can be done with the same logic below without using the END command

BOILER is an output, shows us the boiler is asked to come on.

BOILER-S is an input which shows that the boiler is actually running.

100 IF BOILER THEN START PUMP101 ELSE STOP PUMP101

110 IF NOT BOILER-S THEN STOP PUMP101

---
title: "FOR"
order: 780
---

Purpose:
Looping control. Used in conjunction with NEXT.

Format:
FOR a = x TO y [STEP z]

Description: a is a variable to be used as a counter

x is an expression that is the initial value of the counter y is an expression that is the maximum of the counter z is an expression to be used as an increment

If x is greater than y, when z is positive, or if x is less than y, when z is negative, execution jumps to the statement following the NEXT. Otherwise the lines following the FOR statement are executed until the NEXT statement is en countered. Then the counter is incremented by z. If you do not specify a value for z it is assumed to be 1. If the counter is now greater than y execution continues with the statement following the NEXT. If the counter is equal or less than y,

execution branches back to the statement following the FOR statement. If the value of z is negative, the test is re versed. The counter is decremented, and the loop is executed until the counter is less than y. FOR NEXT loops can be nested up to 10 levels.

Example 1: OUT1 will slowly ramp up to CON1

10 FOR A = 0 TO CON1 STEP .1 20 OUT1 = A

30 WAIT .1

40 NEXT A

Example 2: Check the status of all panels 10 FOR A = 1 TO 12

20 IF STATUS( A ) < > 1 THEN GOSUB 100 30 NEXT A

40 END

50 REM ACTION TO BE TAKEN WHEN BAD PANEL

60 RETURN


---

---
title: "GOSUB"
order: 790
---

Purpose:
To call a subroutine. Used in conjunction with RETURN.

Format:
GOSUB line

Description: line is the line number

EXAMPLE

10 GOSUB 100

20 REM THIS LINE GETS EXECUTED AFTER LINE 120

30 END

100 REM SUBROUTINE GOES HERE

110 A = A + 1

120 RETURN

---
title: "GOTO"
order: 800
---

<table>
  <tr><td><strong>GOTO</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>To redirect the program execution.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>GOTO line</td></tr>
</table>

Purpose: Redirects program flow, jumps from the current line to another line number.

Format: GOTO linenumber

Description: GOTO statement causes the program to jump to the line referred to by linenumber

Example:

10 GOTO 100

20 REM THIS LINE DOES NOT GET EXECUTED

30 REM LINE 120 JUMPS BACK HERE

40 B = B + 1

50 END

100 REM LINE 10 JUMPS THE PROGRAM TO THIS LINE

110 A = A + 1

120 GOTO 30

---
title: "IF"
order: 820
---

IF

Purpose:
Logic statement for decision making.

Format:
IF x THEN clause [ELSE clause]

Description: x is any expression

clause is a BASIC statement or sequence of statements (separated by commas) or a line number to branch to

If x is true (non zero), the clause following the THEN is executed. If x is false (zero), the clause after the ELSE is executed.

<table>
  <tr><td>Example:</td><td>10</td><td>IF OUT1 THEN START OUT2</td></tr>
  <tr><td>20</td><td>IF AVG( A , B , C ) &gt; 22.4 THEN 30 ELSE 50</td></tr>
  <tr><td>30 PRINT “AVERAGE IS ABOVE 22.4"</td></tr>
  <tr><td>40 END</td></tr>
  <tr><td>50 PRINT “AVERAGE IS BELOW 22.4"</td></tr>
  <tr><td>60 END</td></tr>
</table>


---

---
title: "IF+ , IF-"
order: 830
---

<table>
  <tr><td><strong>IF+</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>To detect occurrence of event.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>IF+ x THEN clause [ELSE clause]</td></tr>
</table>

Description: x is any expression

clause is a BASIC statement or sequence of statements (separated by commas), or a line number to branch to

If x is true, and on the previous check it was false, the clause following the THEN is executed. Otherwise the clause after the ELSE is executed.

<table>
  <tr><td>Example:</td><td>10</td><td>IF+ CHILLER THEN GOSUB 40</td></tr>
  <tr><td>20</td><td>IF+ TIME &gt; 12:00 THEN GOSUB 60</td></tr>
  <tr><td>30 END</td></tr>
  <tr><td>40</td><td>PRINT “THE CHILLER HAS JUST COME ON”</td></tr>
  <tr><td>50 RETURN</td></tr>
  <tr><td>60</td><td>REM RING LUNCH BELL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>70</td><td>START BELL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>80</td><td>WAIT 00:00:05</td></tr>
  <tr><td>90</td><td>STOP BELL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>100 RETURN</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td><strong>IF-</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>Purpose:</td><td>To detect occurrence of event.</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>IF- x THEN clause [ELSE clause]</td></tr>
</table>

Description: x is any expression

clause is a BASIC statement or sequence of statements (separated by commas), or a line number to branch to

If x is False, and on the previous check it was true the clause following the THEN is executed. Otherwise the clause after the ELSE is executed.

Example:
10 IF- CHILLER THEN GOSUB 30 20 END

30 PRINT “THE CHILLER HAS JUST GONE OFF”

40 RETURN

INTERVAL

---
title: "ON"
order: 900
---

Purpose:
Selectable GOTO or GOSUB.

Format:
ON n GOTO line [, line...]

ON n GOSUB line [, line...]

Description:
n is an expression rounded to an integer line is a line number in the program

The value of n determines which line number in the list the program uses for branching. For ex am ple, if n is 3, the third line number in the list is the point to which the program branches.

If the value of n is illegal for the given list, no branch occurs.

<table>
  <tr><td>Example:</td><td>10</td><td>VAR1 = AVG ( IN1 , IN2 , IN3 , IN4 ) - 20</td></tr>
  <tr><td>20</td><td>ON VAR1 GOSUB 100 , 200 , 300 , 400 , 500</td></tr>
  <tr><td>30 END</td></tr>
  <tr><td>100</td><td>REM THE AVERAGE IS 21 : RETURN</td></tr>
  <tr><td>200</td><td>REM THE AVERAGE IS 22 : RETURN</td></tr>
  <tr><td>300</td><td>REM THE AVERAGE IS 23 : RETURN</td></tr>
  <tr><td>400</td><td>REM THE AVERAGE IS 24 : RETURN</td></tr>
  <tr><td>500</td><td>REM THE AVERAGE IS 25 : RETURN</td></tr>
</table>

---
title: "PIDProp, PIDInt, PIDDeriv"
order: 940
---

The PIDPROP, PIDRESET and PIDRATE statements allow you to change the PID terms in a control basic program. These commands will be useful for a program which adjusts the PID parameters on the fly such as for a self tuning PID.

A quick summary of the PID terms from the
PID controller discussio
n are shown here for reference:

The proportional term can be thought of as the “rigidity” of the PID loop and its response to a deviation from setpoint. A large value will make the valve or damper lazy while a small term will be more hyper active.

The Integral term accumulates error over time and adds it to the PID loop calculation. A large value for the I term will make the PID more active and a small I term will make the PID loop lazier.

The derivative term allows the PID loop to compensate for a rapidly changing process, if the temperature is quickly approaching setpoint then the D term will increase and slow down the PID response. For common HVAC processes this is left at zero.

![PIDProp, PIDInt, PIDDeriv](../../media/images-drex_module_12_31_screen.png)

<table>
  <tr><td><img src="../../media/images-drex_bullet_1.png" alt="1"><strong>PIDPROP</strong><img src="../../media/images-drex_module_12_31_control_1.png" alt="1. PIDPROP">Purpose: A user defined program can adjust the proportional term of the PID control loops. Format: PIDPROP(PID# , value )Range of the value: 0 TO 4000Description: PID# is the PID loop you wish to change and value is the new proportional term to be set. PIDPROP returns the value that is currently entered into the PID loop proportional term. Example: 10 A = PIDPROP( 1 , 2.3 * SAT * FACTOR )</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td><img src="../../media/images-drex_bullet_2.png" alt="2"><strong>PIDINT</strong><img src="../../media/images-drex_module_12_31_control_2.png" alt="2. PIDINT">Purpose: Enable user programs to change the PID loop integral setting. Format: PIDINT(pid# , value )Range of the Value: 0 to 255Description: pid# is the PID loop you wish to change and value is the new integral value you want to set for this loop. PIDINT returns the current value that is entered into the PID loop Integral term. Example:10 A = PIDINT( 1 , 0.85 )</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td><img src="../../media/images-drex_bullet_3.png" alt="3"><strong>PIDDERIV</strong><img src="../../media/images-drex_module_12_31_control_3.png" alt="3. PIDDERIV">Purpose: Allows programs to change the PID loop derivative setting. Description:PID# is the loop you wish to change the derivative value on, value is the new setting. Format: PIDDERIV(pid# , value )Range for the value: 0 to 2.00PIDDERIV returns the value that is entered into the PID loop derivative setting. Example: 10 IF ABS( SAT - SAS ) &lt; 0.5 THEN A = PIDDERIV( 5 , 50 ) ELSE A = PIDDERIV( 5 , 0 )</td></tr>
</table>

---
title: "PID LOOP MATH DISCUSSION"
order: 950
---

The output value of a controller is calculated according to the following formulas:

OUTPUT = Op + Oi + Od + Ob

Op = Proportional Output = A * 100 / Pb * ( I - S )

Oi = Integral Output = A * R * t/60 * ( I - S ) + Oi

Od = Derivative Output = A * D*(( I - S ) - ( It-1 - St-1 )) / t

Ob = Bias

A = Action (+1 for direct acting, -1 for reverse acting)

Pb = Proportional Band

I = Input

S = Setpoint

R = Reset Constant = Repeats per hour Definition: One repeat per hour will cause an output change of one over one hour for an error of one.

D = Rate Constant = Minutes Definition: One minute will cause an output of one for an error changing by one per minute.

T = Time in Minutes since last scan.

It-1 = Input on last scan.

St-1 = Setpoint on last scan.

Note: Controllers are executed once per second.

Output Limiting Procedure:

1. The sum of Op + Od + Ob is limited to 0-100.

2. Oi is added to the previous sum.

3. The output is again limited to 0-100.

4. Oi is adjusted by the amount that the output was limited in step 3.

Note: Oi is reset to zero when R is set to 0.

Oi is not reset when panel is shut off and on.

---
title: "PHONE"
order: 970
---

PHONE
(T3000 only)

Purpose:
To make connection with a remote computer using a modem.

Format:
PHONE telephone_number_string

Description: string is a string of characters representing the phone number

Example:
10 IF+ TIME >4:00 THEN 15 ELSE 70 15 PHONE 9-555-1234

20 ON-ERROR 100

30 RUN-MACRO 1

40 WAIT 0:10

50 HANGUP

60 END

70 REM CONNECTION NOT MADE

PHONE
(Mini-Panels only)

Purpose:
To allow the Mini-Panel to call a PC running the T3000 software.

Format:
PHONE string

Description: string is a string of characters representing the phone number

In order for the PHONE statement to work all Mini- Panels on the network must have Version 1.91 or higher. The PHONE statement utilizes output 8 of the Mini- Panel which is connected to the modified modem interface box. The modem must be set to not echo characters (ATEO).

Example:
10 REM VAR31 = 1 WHEN CONNECTED

20 REM E = 1 WHEN ERROR TRYING TO CONNECT 30 REM R = RETRY COUNTER

40 REM VAR32 = TIME TO REMAIN CONNECTED , 0 WHEN NO ALARMS 50 IF E THEN GOTO 130

60 IF VAR31 THEN GOTO 200

70 IF VAR32 = 0:00:00 THEN GOTO 220

80 REM CALLOUT ALARMS

90 PHONE ATDT 555-3075

100 ON-ERROR 120

110 VAR31 = 1 : E = 0 : END 120 E = 1 : END

130 REM ERROR SO TRY AGAIN LATER

140 IF R > 20 THEN R = 0 , E = 0 , VAR32 = 0 , END

150 IF TIME-ON( E ) MOD 0:03:00 < 0:00:05 THEN R = R + 1 , GOTO 80 160 GOTO 220

170 REM ALARM SO INCREASE CONNECT TIME

180 IF VAR32 = 0 THEN VAR32 = VAR32 + 0:01:00 ELSE VAR32 = VAR32 + 0:00:15

190 END

200 REM IS IT TIME TO END CALL

210 IF TIME-ON( VAR31 ) > VAR32 THEN VAR31 = 0 , R = 0 , VAR32 = 0 ,

HANGUP

220 REM ALARM STATEMENTS HERE

230 DALARM VAR31 , 5 , ALARMS FROM BOSTON BUILDING 240 ALARM TEMP > SETP , 2 , TEST ALARM

250 ON-ALARM 180


---

title: "POWER-LOSS"
order: 980
---

Purpose: To allow a program to perform some logic determine if there was a power loss to the panel. The function returns 1 the

first time after a power loss, otherwise it returns 0.

Format:
POWER-LOSS

Example:
10 IF POWER-LOSS THEN GOSUB 30 20 END

30 REM INITIALIZATION ROUTINE

40 RETURN

---
title: "PRINT, PRINT-AT"
order: 990
---

PRINT
(T3000 only)

Purpose:
To print strings on a printer

Format:
PRINT string [;]

Description: string is the string to be printed

If a semi colon is included at the end of the PRINT statement no CR/LF (Carriage return / Line feed) will be done, otherwise a CR/LF is done.

Example:

10 IF+ FIRE THEN GOSUB 100

20 END

100 REM FIRE INSTRUCTIONS

110 PRINT “THE FOLLOWING PROCEDURE SHOULD NOW BE

CARRIED OUT:"

120 PRINT “1. ....... etc.”

130 RETURN

Note:
The PRINT-AT statement must be used before using PRINT.

Keywords recognized in PRINT statement:

- DATE
prints the date and time

- TIME
prints the date and time

- USER-A prints the name of user logged on at port A (local)

- USER-B prints the name of user logged on at port A (remote)

- BEEP
causes the printer to beep

- POINT/LOCAL VARIABLE VALUES

prints values from -999,999.9 to 999,999.9

Remember to use the print statement in conjunction with the PRINT- AT statement to specify which panel to print at. If you do not use the PRINT-AT statement, it will not print.

Be careful when the IF+, IF-, and INTER VAL statements are used with PRINT. Make sure that the same item is not printed repeatedly.

Examples:
10 PRINT-AT 2 , 5 10 PRINT-AT 2 , 5

20 IF+ USER-A THEN PRINT USER-A ; “HAS SIGNED ON AT PORT A ON” ; DATE

30 IF+ VALUE > MAXIMUM THEN GOSUB 100

40 IF INTERVAL( 01:00 ) THEN PRINT “BUILDING LOW IS: ; L : ” TIME:" ; TIME

50 END

100 PRINT “VALUE IS” ; VALUE ; “ AT ” ; DATE ; BEEP ; BEEP ; BEEP ; BEEP

110 PRINT “MESSAGE”

120 PRINT “MESSAGE”

130 RETURN

PRINT-AT [TBD]

Purpose:
To select which panel(s) will receive the print message(s).
<table>
  <tr><td>Format:</td><td>PRINT-AT panel [panel panel] or PRINT-AT ALL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Examples:</td><td>10 PRINT-AT ALL</td></tr>
  <tr><td>20</td><td>PRINT “THIS IS PRINTED AT ALL PANELS IN SYSTEM.”</td></tr>
  <tr><td>30</td><td>PRINT-AT 2</td></tr>
  <tr><td>40</td><td>PRINT “THIS IS PRINTED AT PANEL 2 ONLY.”</td></tr>
  <tr><td>50</td><td>PRINT-AT 1 3</td></tr>
  <tr><td>60</td><td>PRINT “THIS IS PRINTED AT PANELS 1 AND 3.”</td></tr>
  <tr><td>70</td><td>PRINT-AT 1 5 8</td></tr>
  <tr><td>80</td><td>PRINT “THIS IS PRINTED AT PANELS 1, 5 AND 8.”</td></tr>
  <tr><td>Note:</td><td>Commas are not needed to separate panel numbers</td></tr>
</table>

---
title: "PTP remote connection"
order: 1000
---

PTP is the connection T3000 makes to a panel that it cannot reach directly. Both ends are behind a router, so neither can open a socket to the other; instead, both talk to a meeting point — the Temco server — and the server tells the host where the panel can be found.

The panel keeps that conversation alive on its own. It is why a controller on a customer site can be reached from an office without a static address, a port forward or a VPN, and why nothing has to be configured on the customer's router.

## The exchange

The whole sequence is a handful of UDP packets. There is no session to keep, no handshake to repeat, and nothing in it is TCP.

1. **Find the server.** The host resolves the meeting point by name (historically `newfirmware.com`) and creates a UDP socket. If the name does not resolve, the attempt ends here with no connection.
2. **Log in.** The host sends a connect command carrying the user name, the password and the serial number of the panel it wants.
3. **Read the answer.** The server either accepts the login or names the reason it did not.
4. **Learn the address.** When the login is accepted, the server returns the panel's current UDP address and port as seen from the outside.
5. **Talk to the panel.** The host sends its packets straight to that address, and from then on the traffic is between the host and the panel, not through the server.
6. **Keep listening.** While the connection is up, incoming packets from the remote address are what T3000 reads; silence means the panel has gone.

## What is sent

The login is one fixed-size structure, so both sides agree on its shape without negotiating anything:

```cpp
struct stLoginMessage
{
    char userName[30];
    char password[20];
};

typedef union
{
    unsigned char all_data[T3000_CONNECT_LENGTH];
    struct
    {
        unsigned int   m_serial_number;   // the panel you want to reach
        stLoginMessage login_message;
        unsigned char  reserved_reg[46];
    } reg_date;
} Str_T3000_Connect;
```

The serial number is the address, not the IP. Two panels may share an address, and one panel may change address between connections — the serial number is the only part that stays true.

## The answers that fail

The server does not simply ignore a bad login; it names the fault, and T3000 shows it:

| Answer | What it means |
|---|---|
| `COMMAND_COMMAND_UNKNOWN` | the server did not recognise the request — usually a mismatched T3000 version |
| `COMMAND_PASSWORD_ERROR` | the user name or password is wrong for that panel |
| `COMMAND_DEVICE_NOT_CONNECT_ERROR` | the panel is not currently registered with the server, so it is switched off or offline |
| `COMMAND_DEVICE_NO_HEARTBEAT_ERROR` | the panel is registered but has stopped answering the server |

The last two are worth telling apart: the first says the controller has never checked in, the second says it has stopped. Both look like "remote connection is not working" from the operator's chair.

## Where the panel is

Once the login is accepted, the address is only a hint until the panel answers a packet. T3000 checks the UDP address and port it was given, sends to it, and waits. If nothing answers at that address, the connection is not established even though the login succeeded — the panel may have moved, or the server's record of it may be stale.

[PTP connection flowchart (PDF)](media/ptp-client-flowchart.pdf)

The chart is the same sequence drawn as a program: socket, login, check, send, listen.

## Related

- [T3000 Remote Connection](menu-items-and-tree/building-database/t3000-remote-connection.md) — the screen this protocol is behind.
- [How T3000 reads and saves data](data-flow.md) — what happens to the values once the connection is up.

---
title: "How T3000 reads and saves data"
order: 1010
---

Every value on screen arrived by one of two routes: it was read from the network, or it was loaded from a database. This page follows one round trip — a scan, a panel chosen in the tree, the points it returns, and where the values then live.

## Finding the panels

A panel has to be discovered before it can be read. Both protocols are broadcast: the host sends one packet to everything on the segment and collects whatever answers.

| Protocol | What the host sends | What a panel answers |
|---|---|---|
| Modbus | `FF 19 FE 01 60 57` | `FF 19`, then its Modbus ID, its serial number and a CRC |
| BACnet | `64 00 00 00 00 00` | `65`, then the device structure |

The answer is what fills in the device record T3000 keeps for every panel it has met:

```cpp
struct refresh_net_device
{
    DWORD          nSerial;              // the identity T3000 uses everywhere
    int            modbusID;
    unsigned short product_id;
    CString        ip_address;
    int            nport;
    float          sw_version;
    float          hw_version;
    unsigned int   object_instance;      // BACnet
    unsigned char  panal_number;
    DWORD          parent_serial_number; // set on an expansion module
    CString        NetCard_Address;
    CString        show_label_name;
    unsigned short bacnetip_port;
};
```

Scanning can start by itself when T3000 opens, or on demand from the tree. Automatic scanning is what makes a panel appear without anyone typing an address; a manual scan is what you reach for when a panel has been replaced and kept its address.

## The tree

The devices that answered are listed in the system tree. Clicking one is the request that follows: T3000 asks that panel for its inputs, its outputs, its variables, and the rest of its table — and the values arrive in one block rather than one point at a time.

That is the reason a large panel takes a moment to open and then fills in quickly: the read is per panel, not per point.

## The read itself

When the panel is already on screen and a value is wanted again, T3000 reads it from the copy it holds in memory rather than going back to the wire. The memory copy is refreshed on demand — by reopening the screen, by a periodic refresh, or by writing a value and reading it back.

This is worth knowing when a reading looks stale: the screen shows what was last read, and the panel's own display may already have moved on.

## Where the values are kept

There are three places a value can be, and they answer different questions:

| Place | Survives a restart | Holds |
|---|---|---|
| the panel | yes — it is the truth | the point's actual value |
| memory in T3000 | no | what was read most recently, for the screens that are open |
| the database | yes | the points, names, ranges and settings that make the panel usable |

Saving to the database is what makes a database project — the panel's points, their names and their configuration — without needing the panel to be online. A panel can be built up in the database first and written to the hardware later.

A `.prg` file is the third form: a single file holding a panel's database record, written and read back from the tree. It is how a panel's setup travels between machines when the panel itself is not present.

## The whole picture

The chart below is the same route as a diagram, from the scan to the two stores:

[Data flow chart (PDF)](media/t3000-data-flow-chart.pdf)

---
title: "WireGuard remote sites"
order: 1020
---

WireGuard carries the same job as [PTP](ptp-client.md) — reaching a panel that is behind a router — with one difference that changes the layout of the feature: a WireGuard gateway is an edge router, so it reaches **an entire building network**, not one controller. What T3000 configures is therefore a *remote site*, not a remote device.

This page describes the design. The screens below are the proposed interface.

## What a remote site holds

A site is one entry describing a gateway and the tunnel to it:

| Field | What it is |
|---|---|
| Remote site name | an identifier, so several properties can be kept apart |
| Public IP address or domain name | the gateway's reachable WAN address |
| WireGuard port | the port the gateway listens on; 51820 by default |
| VPN IP address | the address inside the tunnel |
| Public and private keys | the key pair that identifies each end |
| Pre-shared key | optional, an extra layer on top of the keys |
| Connection status | Idle, Connecting, Connected, or Error |

The key pair is generated by T3000 and not typed. That is the point of the feature: a technician never edits a configuration file, and the private key never has to be handed to anyone.

## Provisioning a gateway

The parameters have to reach the hardware somehow, and the hardware is on the far side of the link that is not up yet. The design solves this by provisioning over the local Modbus RTU connection first:

1. **Create the site** in the T3000 profile.
2. **Define the server side** — the public port and the subnets the tunnel will carry.
3. **Generate both key pairs**, host and gateway, in one step.
4. **Push the parameter stack over Modbus RTU** — the gateway's private key, T3000's public key, the tunnel addresses and the port rules — written to the gateway's own non-volatile memory.
5. **Register the client** in the server list once the download succeeds.
6. **Start the tunnel** on the host PC.

Steps 1 to 5 need no tunnel, which is the whole reason the order works: the local serial link carries the settings that make the remote link possible.

## The two screens

The server screen is the master view: whether the service is running, the server's public key, and the client matrix — each endpoint with its assigned tunnel address and its WAN endpoint.

![](media/wireguard-server-configuration.png)

The gateway screen is the deployment view, deliberately reduced to two actions: generate the keys, then press **Provision Device via Modbus RTU**. Everything it pushes is inherited from the server screen rather than typed again.

![](media/wireguard-gateway-provisioning.png)

## Running the tunnel

T3000 owns the lifecycle rather than leaving it to a service console. It assembles the configuration from the database fields, starts, pauses and stops the interface, polls the endpoint for uptime and byte counters, and reconnects with a back-off when a link drops.

The status the operator sees is therefore one of five things, and the last two are worth keeping apart:

| Status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Connected | the tunnel is up and the endpoint answers |
| Connecting | the interface is up, the handshake has not completed |
| Disconnected | stopped, or the link was lost |
| Authentication error | the keys do not match — the tunnel will never come up until they do |
| Configuration error | the parameters themselves are wrong |

## What the site sees

Once the tunnel is up the gateway is a transparent bridge: it receives the encrypted traffic from the T3000 host, decapsulates it, and routes it to the internal network unchanged. To the point table, a BACnet/IP controller behind the gateway behaves like one on the local segment — the protocol is not wrapped or rewritten at either end.

![](media/wireguard-secure-access.png)

## Status

This is a proposed framework, not a released feature. The field list, the provisioning order and the status values above are the contract the implementation is being built against; treat a T3000 that shows no WireGuard screen as current behaviour rather than a fault.

## Related

- [PTP remote connection](ptp-client.md) — the connection this replaces for whole-site access.
- [IP Settings](ip-settings.md) — addressing on the local segment.

title: "TIME-ON, TIME-OFF"
order: 1100
---

Purpose:
Used in programs to trigger events based on the duration of time an item has been on or off.

Use only with items which are binary such as ON-OFF and START-STOP for the range.

Can be used with outputs, inputs, variables, schedules

TIME-ON

Purpose:
Returns the length of time that an item has been on

Format:
TIME-ON( item )

Example:
10 REM START OUT2 10 MINUTES AFTER OUT1 IS ON

20 IF TIME-ON( OUT1 ) > 0:10 THEN START OUT2

TIME-OFF

Purpose:
Returns the length of time that an item has been off

Format:
TIME-OFF( item )

Example:
10 START OUT1 FOR 20 MINUTES, THEN STOP FOR 20 MINUTES, REPEATING

20 IF TIME-OFF( OUT1 ) > 0:20 THEN START OUT1

30 IF TIME-ON( OUT1 ) > 0:20 THEN STOP OUT1

---
title: "The programming DLL"
order: 1110
---

T3000 can hand a Control Basic program to another program — to encode it for storage, and to decode it back — through a small DLL that ships beside `T3000.exe`. The same DLL is referred to in Temco's sources as the *programming DLL* and as the *interpreter DLL*; it is one library, and this page covers both names.

The library lives in `T3000ProgramMoudle` and is built as `T3000ProgramMoudle.dll`, with `T3000ProgramMoudle.lib` for linking.

## Calling it from your own program

Three steps, and the first one is the one people forget.

1. **Add the import library.** In the project's properties, Linker → Input → Additional Dependencies, add `T3000ProgramMoudle.lib` — the same place `sqlite3.lib` and `HttpApi.lib` are already listed.
2. **Put the DLL where the program looks for it.** In the T3000 tree that is the run directory beside `T3000.exe`, alongside `T3000ProgramMoudle.lib`, `.exp` and `.pdb`.
3. **Include the header.** `dllmain.h` declares the two entry points; including it is what makes the calls compile.

![](media/dll-dependencies.png)

![](media/dll-run-directory.png)

## The two calls

Encoding turns program text into the array T3000 stores; decoding turns that array back into text. Both are `extern "C"` and exported, so the names are not mangled and can be found by any caller:

```cpp
extern "C" __declspec(dllexport) int   Encode_function(char* input_text, encode_str* encodestr);
extern "C" __declspec(dllexport) char* Decode_function(char* input_code, decode_str* decodestr);
```

Each call reports through a structure, and each structure carries the result **and** a message — which is the part that makes a failure readable rather than a return code:

```cpp
typedef struct
{
    int  errorcode;
    char encode_array[2000];    // the encoded program
    char encode_message[255];   // why it failed, if it did
} encode_str;

typedef struct
{
    int  errorcode;
    char decode_text[4000];     // the decoded program
    char decode_message[255];   // why it failed, if it did
} decode_str;
```

The arrays are fixed sizes, and that is the one limit worth designing around: an encoded program is capped at 2000 characters and a decoded one at 4000.

## A worked call

```cpp
#include "..\T3000ProgramMoudle\dllmain.h"

char test_program[] = "10 REM TEST PROGRAM \r\n20 VAR = VAR1 + 1\r\n";
char ret_value[2000] = { 0 };

encode_str Myencodestr;
int encode_ret = 0;

encode_ret = Encode_function(test_program, &Myencodestr);
if (encode_ret != -1)
{
    MessageBox(_T("Decode error!"));
}

char* decode_ret = NULL;
decode_str Mydecode_str;

decode_ret = Decode_function(Myencodestr.encode_array, &Mydecode_str);
if (decode_ret == NULL)
{
    MessageBox(_T("Decode Failed!"));
}
```

Round-tripping the same text through both calls is the fastest way to check that the DLL is the right build: the decoded text should match what was handed in, line for line.

## Status

The DOCX this page comes from is a two-page note with the same content under both file names (`HowToUseDll.docx` and `HowToUseT3000 Interpreter DLL.docx` in `T3000ProgramMoudle`), so the two topics the older help tree listed are one topic here.

## Related

- [Programming a controller](programming-a-controller.md) — writing a program from the screens.
- [Control Basic Programming](control-basic-programming-overview.md) — the language being encoded.

---
title: "TIME"
order: 1110
---

Purpose: The TIME statement enables programs to use time in the calculations in an easy to read format. TIME returns the time as a number with hours as the thousands and hundreds digits, minutes are the ones and tens digits. Seconds are stored after the decimal place in the first and second decimals.

Note: If the seconds are not specified, the time will be interpreted as hours:minutes and the seconds are assumed as zero.

12:30:05=1250.05

12:30 = 1230

Times are evaluated to real numbers using the following formula:

time = hour*100 + minutes + seconds / 100

The time of 14:30:30 is returned as a real number of value = 1430.30

EXAMPLE: 10 IF TIME = 13:00:05 THEN START PUMP1

20 IF+ PUMP1 THEN VAR6 = TIME

30 REM VAR6 CONTAINS THE TIME THAT PUMP1 STARTED

---
title: "USER[X]"
order: 1130
---

Purpose: Provides information about which user is logged on and through which port.

The function will checks all ports and the various communications ports for logged in users and return the user ID for any matches.

Format: USER[X]

Where X is the port number through which the user has logged in.

0 = nobody logged in

1 = checks users from all ports

2 = Ethernet port

3 = Main RS485 port

4 = Subnet RS485 port

5 = RS232 port (available on the T3-BB model only) [TBD]

6 = GSM port , only applicable if the unit is equipped with a GSM module.

The function returns the user ID from the list of users defined in the user list.

10 REM SEND A PERSONAL MESSAGE TO USER NO. 5

20 IF+ USER[6] = 5 THEN GOSUB 40

30 END

40 PRINT “HELLO TO USER#5 SIGNED IN OVER THE GSM LINK”

50 RETURN

</table>

Note:

This statement is handy for controlling timing sequences. Other programs in the panel will not be affected, only the program where the statement appears will be suspended.

---
title: "ErrorMessages"
order: 1160
---

## 10.5 ERROR MESSAGES

When the programming changes are done the user hits the F2 key or "SEND" from the menu system to send the changes to the controller. The software checks the program for errors and if all is clear sends the program to the controller. If errors are found the program is not sent and the errors are displayed in a popup and also at the bottom of the editor.

![](../media/images-drex_module_12_51_custom.png)

## 10.5.1 SYNTAX ERRORS

Syntax errors usually are caused by spelling mistakes of syntax errors.

Here is a list of the error messages:

<table>
  <tr><td>1.</td><td>Expecting variable</td><td>7.</td><td>Illegal extra characters on line</td></tr>
  <tr><td>2.</td><td>Expecting =</td><td>8.</td><td>Expecting another expression</td></tr>
  <tr><td>3.</td><td>Expecting operator</td><td>9.</td><td>Illegal line number in goto</td></tr>
  <tr><td>4.</td><td>Bracket error</td><td>10.</td><td>String is missing end quote (“)</td></tr>
  <tr><td>5.</td><td>Not variable on left of equals</td><td>11.</td><td>Attempt to change the value of a point in another panel</td></tr>
  <tr><td>6.</td><td>Function argument not expression</td></tr>
</table>

<table>
  <tr><td>10.5.2 <strong>STRUCTURAL ERRORS</strong></td></tr>
  <tr><td>1.</td><td>Program too large —- text buffer</td><td>7.NEXT without FOR in line ###</td></tr>
  <tr><td>2.</td><td>Too many GOTOs —- allowed 50</td><td>8.Too many FOR's —- allowed 10</td></tr>
  <tr><td>3.</td><td>Program too large at line ###</td><td>9.Expecting line number after line ###</td></tr>
  <tr><td>4.</td><td>Too many lines —- allowed 100</td><td>10. Line after ### is too long</td></tr>
</table>

5. FOR without NEXT

6. GOTO or GOSUB to undefined line ###


---

---
title: "Port Forwarding"
order: 1170
---

*Port Forwarding

Following are the steps for port forwarding:

Step1.
Set port forward for router:TCP 502(modbus TCP);UDP 47808(BIP port); UDP 1234(Temco private scan).

Step2.Click to show the tab as below,select “Remote Device”protocol.

![](media/images-drex_module_13_custom.png)

Step3.It will show the tab as below, click to fil outer net IP or domain name.

![](media/images-drex_module_13_custom_2.png)

Step4.Scan via T3000 software,the remote device can be connected.

![](media/images-drex_module_13_custom_3.png)

![](media/images-drex_module_13_custom_4.png)


---

---
title: "Network&Remote points support"
order: 1180
---

T3-serial controller (only new ARM) can support network points and remote points.

1.
Network points function

For example,there are panel 1 and 2 in network. We can use 2.2.VARx 2.2.OUTx 2.2.Inx to read or write the network ponts.Range of x is 1 to 255.

2.
Remote Bacnet points

For example,panel 1 is T3000 controller, device 3 is MSTP slave device,which is integrated in only master RS485 port, and this port is set to master MSTP function.We can use 1.3.AVx 1.3.Aix 1.3.DOx 1.3.AOx to read and write remote bacnet points.Range of x is 1 to 255.

3.
Remote Modbus points

For example,panel 2 is controller, device 4 is Modbus slave device, which is integrated in master or slave RS485 port, and this port is set to master modbus function.We can use 2.4.MB_REGx or 2.4.REGx to read and write remote modbus points. MB_REG is base 0, and REG is base1.Range of x is 0 to 2047.

![](media/images-drex_module_15_custom.png)

Ver 2016.4.14

3 Check all devices in the building for new firmware and allow user to update any or all

2 Auto detect the braudrate during network discovery

Ver Rev2016.3.18

1 Firmware updates with ISPTool checks for high baud rate, updates with high baud rate, then sets it back to the original setting.

prevent user from loading bad files to the device.

and update the node in the system tree.

If you get the latest firmware from temco's engineer, don't need the step 1.

2> If your computer didn't install the T3000 ,please download
T3000Software.zip
.

---
title: "14 How to use subnet"
order: 1230
---

Description:

All subnets connected to MP can be used in the MP ,directly .

![](media/images-drex_module_19_custom.png)

Format :

1-48-VAR101

Description : This is a register value of the Modbus ID 48 connected to pannel ID 1

Panel ID =1 (What's Panel ID ,please refer to
**Chapter 7**
7.1.5
)

Subnet Modbus ID =48

VAR101 : Register Number is 101

Notes : Register Number is PLC Address (Base 1). If you use Protocol Address (Base 0),

you must plus one to your current register number .

How to use in program:

EXAMPLE 1:GET THE VALUE OF THE REGISTER VALUE OF A SUBNET

10 REM *** THE VALUE OF 1-48-VAR101 ASSIGN TO VAR1 ***

20 VAR1 = 1-48-VAR101

EXAMPLE 1:SET THE VALUE OF THE REGISTER VALUE OF A SUBNET

10 REM *** VAR1 ASSIGN TO THE VALUE OF 1-48-VAR101 ***

20 1-48-VAR101 = VAR1

NOTE: When you use the register number ,you must know whether the register number can have the property of R/W.

Example show :

10 VAR1 = 1-48-VAR101

20 VAR2 = 1-48-VAR102

30 VAR3 = 1-47-VAR101

40 VAR4 = 1-47-VAR102

50 VAR5 = 1-20-VAR101

60 VAR6 = 1-20-VAR102

70 VAR7 = 1-19-VAR101

80 VAR8 = 1-19-VAR102

90 VAR9 = 1-44-VAR101

100 VAR10 = 1-44-VAR102

110 VAR11 = 1-46-VAR101

120 VAR12 = 1-46-VAR102

130 VAR13 = 1-49-VAR101

140 VAR14 = 1-49-VAR102

150 REM *** START COOLING PROCESS ***

160 IF VAR3 OR VAR5 OR VAR 7 OR VAR9 > 150 THEN START OUT1 , START OUT32

170 IF IN2 &lt;= 160 THEN START OUT4, OUT5, OUT6, OUT7, OUT8, OUT9, OUT10, OUT11, OUT12, OUT13, OUT14

180 IF IN3 >= 700 THEN START OUT2

190 IF IN3 >= 850 THEN STOP OUT32 , STOP OUT2 , STOP OUT1

200 IF IN2 &lt;= 20 THE STOP OUT32 , STOP OUT1

220 IF VAR3 = 100 THEN STOP OUT4 , STOP OUT11

230 IF VAR5 = 100 THEN STOP OUT5 , STOP OUT12

240 IF VAR7 = 100 THEN STOP OUT6 , STOP OUT13

250 IF VAR9 = 100 THEN STOP OUT7 , STOP OUT14

260 REM *** START COOLING PROCESS ROOM1 & ROOM6 ***

270 IF REM VAR1 OR VAR11 OR VAR13 > 240 THEN START OUT1 , START OUT32

280 IF IN2 &lt; 200 THEN START OUT3, OUT8, OUT9, OUT10, OUT15, OUT16

290 IF IN3 >= 700 THEN START OUT2

300 IF IN3 >= 900 THEN STOP OUT32 , THEN STOP OUT2 , STOP OUT1

310 IF IN2 &lt;= 20 THE STOP OUT32 , STOP OUT1

320 IF VAR11 > 240 THEN START OUT3 , START OUT10

330 IF VAR11 = 200 THEN STOP OUT3 , STOP OUT10

320 IF VAR11 > 240 THEN START OUT8 , START OUT15

330 IF VAR11 = 200 THEN STOP OUT8 , STOP OUT15

340 IF VAR13 > 240 THEN START OUT9 , START OUT16

350 IF VAR13 = 200 THEN STOP OUT9 , STOP OUT16

360 REM *** START HUMIDIFIER PROCESS ***

380 IF VAR2 OR VAR4 OR VAR6 OR VAR 8 OR VAR10 OR VAR12 &lt; 850 THEN START OUT2 ,START (OUT17 TO OUT23) , START (OUT10 TO OUT16)

390 IF IN3 &lt; 450 THEN START OUT1 , START OUT32

400 WHEN IN3 = 700 THEN STOP OUT1 , STOP OUT32

410 IF VAR2 OR VAR4 OR VAR6 OR VAR 8 OR VAR10 OR VAR12 > 950 THEN STOP OUT32 , OUT2

title: "Expansion Inputs & Outputs"
order: 1240
---

Description:

The T3 controllers can be expanded with external input / output modules.

The expansion points can be addressed the same as the on board i/o by referring to the input or output ID such as IN25 or OUT25. As of this writing, early 2017 the T3 controllers can be expanded to a maximum of 64 inputs and 64 outputs, this will be expanded to 128 inputs and 128 outputs in the very near term [TBD: update this as soon as this firmware is available].

<table>
  <tr><td>TYPE OF T3</td><td>INNER INPUTS</td><td>INNER DIGITE OUTPUTS</td><td>INNER ANALOG OUTPUTS</td><td>OUTER INPUTS</td><td>OUTER OUTPUTS</td></tr>
  <tr><td>MP24</td><td>32</td><td>12</td><td>12</td><td>32</td><td>40</td></tr>
  <tr><td>MP10</td><td>16</td><td>6</td><td>4</td><td>48</td><td>54</td></tr>
  <tr><td>MP8</td><td>8</td><td>6</td><td>2</td><td>56</td><td>56</td></tr>
</table>

From the above table ,we can see the different MP support the different number of inner IO .I will show an example of MP24 connecting a T3-8I13O

You can know other types MP, in the same way .

---
title: "Offline Mode"
order: 1280
---

T3000 has a feature where you can operate on a T3 controller in offline mode. This is useful when you don’t have a controller on hand for demo purposes or working in the office for later deployment to a remote job site.Currently only T3 controllers are supported.Below is an explanation on how to operate on a virtual controller.

First, load into the T3 controller in offline mode as the link
http://tinyurl.com/ybaj9d3u/ExamplePRGFiles.zip
,and set T3000 to offline mode from the menu system.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom.png)![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_2.png)

The currently selected device showing at Tab 1 will start flashing red-blue-green to signify that it is no longer online. Right click on the ‘Virtual Device’ icon at Tab 2.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_3.png)

Which brings up two choices for sorting the tree and the ‘Add virtual device’ option which is what we’re interested in.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_4.png)

Many devices show up in the next dialog which pops up. The only working one at present is the T3 controller series, BB, LB and TB programmable controllers.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_5.png)

Next load an example file into the virtual controller.You will see the status of the file loading down at the bottom of the screen.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_6.png)
![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_7.png)

After loading the ‘prg’ file you can view the inputs, outputs, programming and so on of the virtual controller as if you were online with actual hardware.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_8.png)

After editing you should save your changes using the File -> Save menu item and select a folder & name for the updated program. The file is stored as a ‘prg’ or program file for backup purposes, for copying to another controller or be emailed to someone. The PRG file contains all the i/o point definitions, programming, user screens and so on which a controller uses to perform its job.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_9.png)

A good naming convention helps keep track of the most recent version of the file while still keeping the earlier versions on hand in case of problems.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_10.png)

The only files which the PRG file does not contain are the graphics images which need to be copied over manually to any other PC.’s which will be accessing the system. In this example, DemoSite1 is currently the selected building. The image files are stored in the folder shown at Tab 2, in this case the folder is called C:\Program Files (x86)\T3000\Database\Buildings\DemoSite1\image. All image files for this particular site are stored in this folder. Tab 3 shows the pictures are in the ‘image’ subfolder.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_11.png)

While it is possible to add a second virtual device, the system only allows you to operate on one at a time. Changes made to one virtual controller will affect the other virtual controllers. TBD: allow multiple virtual devices.

![](media/images-drex_module_25_custom_12.png)


---

---
title: "Glossary"
order: 1340
---

The words this manual uses in a particular way. Where a term also has an everyday meaning, the entry gives the meaning T3000 gives it.

## The system

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Panel** | A controller on the network. Used interchangeably with *controller* and *device*; a panel holds the points, the programs and the database record. |
| **T3** | The Temco Controls controller family. A T3 is the panel most of this manual describes. |
| **TStat8** | The Temco Controls thermostat. It has its own points and schedules and is documented separately. |
| **Expansion module** | A board that adds points to a panel — `T3-8O` for outputs, `T3-22I` for inputs, `T3-PT12` for temperature. It carries its own serial number and names its parent. |
| **Subnet** | The group of panels reachable on one network segment. Panels are found and read by scanning their subnet. |
| **Serial number** | The identity of a panel. Everything T3000 keeps about a panel is keyed by it, and it does not change when the panel's address does. |
| **Modbus ID** | The address a panel answers to on a serial Modbus segment. Two panels on one segment cannot share one. |
| **BACnet instance** | The object number a device answers to on a BACnet network, in the same role the Modbus ID plays on Modbus. |
| **Scan** | Asking the network what is there. A scan can run automatically when T3000 opens or on demand. |
| **PTP** | The connection T3000 uses to reach a panel through the Temco server when neither end can be dialled directly. |

## Points

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Point** | One addressable value on a panel — an input, an output or a variable. |
| **Input** | A value read from the panel's terminals: a temperature, a contact, a pulse count. |
| **Output** | A value the panel drives: a relay, a 0–10 V signal, a triac. |
| **Variable** | A value the panel holds for its own use — a setpoint, a counter, a flag. |
| **Analog / digital** | Whether a point carries a number or is on or off. |
| **Range** | The engineering meaning of an input's raw value: what a reading of 4 mA or 500 counts represents. |
| **Custom digital range** | A digital range with a label of its own, so a contact reads as *Filter dirty* rather than *Off*. |
| **Pulse counting** | Counting digital transitions over time instead of reporting a state. |
| **Point type** | Which of the three tables a point lives in: `INPUT`, `OUTPUT` or `VARIABLE`. |

## Control

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Control Basic** | The panel's programming language. Programs are written in numbered lines, in the style of BASIC. |
| **Program** | A Control Basic program held by a panel. A panel runs its programs once per scan. |
| **PRG file** | A file holding a panel's database record — points, names, ranges, settings — so it can be carried between machines without the panel. |
| **PID loop** | A control loop that corrects an output from the error between a setpoint and a measured value, using proportional, integral and derivative terms. |
| **Setpoint** | The value a loop is trying to reach. |
| **Process variable** | The measured value a loop is trying to hold at the setpoint. |
| **Schedule** | A weekly program: which hours of which days a panel does something. Repeats every seven days. |
| **Annual routine** | The year's exceptions — holidays and one-off closures — applied on top of the weekly schedules. |
| **Auto / manual** | Whether a point is driven by the panel's own logic (*auto*) or held at a value somebody typed (*manual*). A manual point ignores its program. |

## Records

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Trend log** | A record of a point's value over time, stored so the history can be read back and graphed. |
| **Alarm** | A condition the panel reports when a point crosses a limit, with the time it happened and whether it was acknowledged. |
| **Graphic screen** | A drawing of the plant with live points placed on it, so a system can be read at a glance. |
| **Building database** | The stored copy of every panel's points and settings. It exists whether or not the panels are online, which is what allows a site to be built before the hardware is. |
| **Offline mode** | Working with the building database when no panel is reachable. |
| **Write / read back** | Sending a value or a table to a panel, then reading it to confirm what the panel actually holds. |

## Connections

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **COM port** | A serial port on the PC or the panel. T3000 numbers them `COM0`, `COM1`, `COM2`. |
| **MSTP** | BACnet over a serial segment — the BACnet equivalent of Modbus RTU. |
| **BBMD** | A BACnet broadcast management device, which lets broadcast traffic cross between IP subnets. |
| **Port forwarding** | A router rule that lets a connection from outside reach a device inside. Needed for direct access to a panel. |
| **VPN** | A tunnel that makes a remote network reachable as though it were local. |
| **WireGuard** | The VPN protocol T3000 is being built to configure itself, per remote site rather than per panel. |
| **Firmware** | The software inside a panel. Updating it is a maintenance task, and the update is checked by version. |
